A Coalgebraic Approach to Dualities for Neighborhood Frames
Guram Bezhanishvili, Nick Bezhanishvili, Jim de Groot ORCID:0000-0003-1375-6758

Abstract
. We develop a uniform coalgebraic approach to Jónsson-Tarski and Thomason type dualities for various classes of neighborhood frames and neighborhood algebras. In the first part of the paper we construct an endofunctor on the category of complete and atomic Boolean algebras that is dual to the double powerset functor on 𝖲𝖾𝗍 \mathsf{Set} . This allows us to show that Thomason duality for neighborhood frames can be viewed as an algebra-coalgebra duality. We generalize this approach to any class of algebras for an endofunctor presented by one-step axioms in the language of infinitary modal logic. As a consequence, we obtain a uniform approach to dualities for various classes of neighborhood frames, including monotone neighborhood frames, pretopological spaces, and topological spaces. In the second part of the paper we develop a coalgebraic approach to Jónsson-Tarski duality for neighborhood algebras and descriptive neighborhood frames. We introduce an analogue of the Vietoris endofunctor on the category of Stone spaces and show that descriptive neighborhood frames are isomorphic to coalgebras for this endofunctor. This allows us to obtain a coalgebraic proof of the duality between descriptive neighborhood frames and neighborhood algebras. Using one-step axioms in the language of finitary modal logic, we restrict this duality to other classes of neighborhood algebras studied in the literature, including monotone modal algebras and contingency algebras. We conclude the paper by connecting the two types of dualities via canonical extensions, and discuss when these extensions are functorial.

1.
Introduction

Categorical dualities linking algebra and topology (such as Stone’s seminal duality for Boolean algebras
[
Sto36
]
and distributive lattices
[
Sto38
]
) have been of fundamental importance in the development of mathematics, logic, and theoretical computer science (see for example
[
Joh82
,
BRV01
,
GHK+80
]
). With algebras corresponding to the syntactic side of logical systems and topological spaces to the semantic side, Stone type duality theory provides a powerful mathematical framework for studying various properties of logical systems. That duality theory plays an important rôle in computer science was emphasized by Plotkin
[
Plo83
]
and Smyth
[
Smy83
]
who pointed out that the duality between state-transformer and predicate-transformer semantics is an instance of a Stone type duality, as well as by Abamsky
[
Abr91
]
who investigated the connection between program logic and domain theory via Stone duality. The latter is an example of a duality between operational/denotational semantics and program logic. More recently, topological dualities have also been fruitfully explored in understanding minimization of various types of automata
[
BKP12
,
BBH+14
,
BBH+20
]
, in classification of regular languages
[
GGP08
,
Geh16b
]
, and in analyzing probabilistic systems
[
KLMP13
,
FKL+17
]
. We refer to
[
Pan13
]

and
[
Geh16a
]
for overviews of the use of duality theory in computer science. Modern applications of duality theory involve algebras enriched with extra operators.
This can be placed in the context of a duality between algebras for a functor and coalgebras for its dual functor
[
Ven07
]
, providing yet another connection with computer science applications as coalgebra is known to be a theory of evolving systems
[
Rut00
,
Jac16
]
. In this paper we develop a new uniform coalgebraic approach to Jónsson-Tarski and Thomason type dualities for various classes of neighborhood frames and neighborhood algebras. By doing this we are expanding the toolbox of duality theory for computer science.

Jónsson-Tarski duality establishes that the category of modal algebras is dually equivalent to the category of descriptive Kripke frames, while Thomason duality establishes a dual equivalence between the category of Kripke frames and the category of complete and atomic modal algebras
(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
such that
is completely multiplicative. Jónsson-Tarski duality generalizes Stone duality between Boolean algebras and Stone spaces (compact Hausdorff zero-dimensional spaces), and Thomason duality generalizes Tarski duality between complete and atomic Boolean algebras and sets.

A coalgebraic proof of Jónsson-Tarski duality is obtained by lifting Stone duality to an appropriate duality of functors
[
Abr88
,
KKV04
,
KKP05
]
.
More precisely, let
\mathsf{Stone}
be the category of Stone spaces and
\mathfun{V}:\mathsf{Stone}\to\mathsf{Stone}
the Vietoris endofunctor on
\mathsf{Stone}
. Then the category
\mathsf{DKF}
of descriptive Kripke frames is isomorphic to the category
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})
of coalgebras for
\mathfun{V}
. In
[
KKV04
]
an endofunctor
\mathfun{K}:\mathsf{BA}\to\mathsf{BA}
on the category
\mathsf{BA}
of Boolean algebras is constructed that is dual to
\mathfun{V}
. Consequently, the category
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})
of algebras for
\mathfun{K}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})
. Since
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})
is isomorphic to the category
\mathsf{MA}
of modal algebras, putting the pieces together yields Jónsson-Tarski duality, stating that
\mathsf{MA}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{DKF}
.

Recently a similar approach was undertaken to prove Thomason duality. It is well known that the category
\mathsf{KF}
of Kripke frames is isomorphic to the category
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{P})
of coalgebras for the covariant powerset endofunctor
\mathfun{P}
on the category
\mathsf{Set}
of sets. In
[
BCM22
]
an endofunctor
\mathfun{H}
is constructed on the category
\mathsf{CABA}
of complete and atomic Boolean algebras that is dual to
\mathfun{P}
. Let
\mathsf{CAMA}
be the category of complete and atomic modal algebras
(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
such that
is completely multiplicative. Then
\mathsf{CAMA}
is isomorphic to the category
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})
of algebras for
\mathfun{H}
, and we arrive at Thomason duality.

In summary, we have two main dualities for normal modal logic, the “topological” duality of Jónsson and Tarski, and the “discrete” duality of Thomason.
There is a tradeoff in the complexity of the structures involved in these dualities. In Jónsson-Tarski duality the algebra side of the duality is “simple” (finitary), but the frame side is complex (the relational structures need to be equipped with a Stone topology resulting in descriptive Kripke frames). On the other hand, in Thomason duality the frame side of the duality is simple (Kripke frames), while the algebra side is complex (infinitary).

Jónsson-Tarski duality and Thomason duality are related to each other by the following commutative diagram.
The diagram on the right is a copy of that on the left, but with the modal algebras and frames presented as algebras and coalgebras for the appropriate functors.
The algebraic counterpart of the forgetful functor

\mathfun{U}:\mathsf{DKF}\to\mathsf{KF}
is given by the canonical extension functor

\sigma:\mathsf{MA}\to\mathsf{CAMA}
making the diagram on the left commute.
(We use
\equiv^{\op}
to indicate dual equivalence.)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DKF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CAMA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{KF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}

(1)

We encounter a similar situation when looking at non-normal modal logic—an extension of classical propositional logic with a unary modal operator
that satisfies only the
congruence rule. Its algebras are Boolean algebras with an arbitrary endofunction.
We call these
neighborhood algebras
and write
\mathsf{NA}
for
the category of neighborhood algebras and corresponding homomorphisms.
The standard geometric semantics of non-normal modal logic is given by
neighborhood frames
,
discovered independently by Scott
[
Sco70
]
and Montague

[
Mon70
]
(see also
[
Che80
,
Pac17
]
). Neighborhood frames can be represented as
coalgebras for the double contravariant powerset functor on
\mathsf{Set}
(see, e.g.,
[
Ven07
, Example 9.5]
).
We write
\mathsf{NF}
for the category of neighborhood frames and appropriate
morphisms.
Došen
[
Doš89
]
generalized both Jónsson-Tarski and
Thomason dualities to the setting of neighborhood algebras and frames.
The former gives rise to the notion of
descriptive neighborhood
frames
, and the latter yields
complete atomic neighborhood algebras
.
If we write
\mathsf{DNF}
and
\mathsf{CANA}
for the respective categories, then we obtain the following analogue of Diagram (
1
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{NA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DNF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CANA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{NF}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}

(2)

But the analogy is not perfect: in the non-normal case
there are two natural versions of the canonical extension, the
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions, that do not coincide. Moreover, neither is functorial. In fact,
\sigma:\mathsf{MA}\to\mathsf{CAMA}
is a functor because the
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions coincide in the presence of normality.
These two extensions have been investigated
in the setting of distributive lattices
[
GJ94
,
GJ04
]
, arbitrary lattices
[
GH01
]
, and even posets
[
GP08
,
GJP13
]
.
In Section
6
we will discuss how to obtain functoriality of
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions in some special cases.

An important subcategory of
\mathsf{NA}
is that of
monotone neighborhood frames
. For this subcategory, Hansen
[
Han03
]
and Hansen and Kupke
[
HK04
]
developed an alternative approach to Došen duality. In fact, Hansen’s descriptive neighborhood frames are
\sigma
-extensions of Došen’s descriptive neighborhood frames. While Hansen and Kupke mention a coalgebraic approach to Došen duality, they do not go as far as to prove Došen duality using the coalgebraic approach discussed above.
In Section
6.3
we will see how to obtain such a proof from our approach, which we next outline.

Our main goal is to give a uniform (predominantly coalgebraic)
approach to dualities for neighborhood frames. We construct an endofunctor

\mathfun{L}
on the category
\mathsf{CABA}
of complete atomic Boolean algebras (CABAs)
that is dual to the double powerset functor.
This endofunctor is obtained by modding out the free CABA over a set by the
axioms of
\mathsf{CANA}
.
The above construction presumes existence of free CABAs. It is well known that free complete Boolean algebras do not exist
[
Gai64
,
Hal64
]
.
However, free CABAs do exist. This follows from the fact that the Eilenberg-Moore algebras of the double contravariant powerset monad are exactly
CABAs
[
Tay02
]
, and that categories of algebras for monads have free objects
[
AHS90
, Proposition 20.7(2)]
. A concrete description of free CABAs was given in
[
BCM22
]
.
We generalize this
to any subcategory of
\mathsf{CANA}

axiomatized by one-step axioms in the language of infinitary modal logic.
As a consequence, we obtain a uniform approach to duality theory for classes of neighborhood frames, including monotone
neighborhood frames, filter frames, and neighborhood contingency frames.
Additional correspondence results then give rise to dualities for
pretopological spaces, topological spaces, and their various subcategories.

In the second part of the paper we define an analogue of the Vietoris endofunctor on Stone spaces
and show that coalgebras for this endofunctor are exactly the descriptive neighborhood
frames.
This allows us to obtain a coalgebraic proof of the duality between descriptive
neighborhood frames and neighborhood algebras. Furthermore, using one-step axioms in the language of finitary modal
logic, we restrict this duality to other classes of neighborhood algebras studied in the literature such as normal modal algebras and contingency algebras.

This restriction does not always correspond to known dualities.
For example, when restricting neighborhood algebras to monotone Boolean algebra
expansions (BAMs)
[
Han03
, Chapter 7]
we do not obtain the duality for
monotone modal logic of Hansen and Kupke
[
HK04
]
.
In fact, the neighborhood frames underlying the descriptive frames in the duality
obtained from our general theory need not be monotone.
The descriptive frames used by Hansen and Kupke can be obtained
from ours through the theory of canonical extensions.
In Section
6
we provide an axiom which guarantees
that this construction is functorial.
We use this to give an alternative coalgebraic proof of the duality for BAMs
in
[
HK04
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Duality theory for normal modal logics

Classical modal logic is an extension of classical propositional logic
with an additional unary modality
.
The modality
is called
normal
if it distributes over finite meets.
By the standard interpretation in a Kripke frame
(X,R)
, a state
x
in
X

satisfies

-successors satisfy

.

iff it

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

.
To interpret a non-normal
we need to generalize Kripke semantics to
the so-called neighborhood semantics.

{defi}

[
[
Che80
,
Pac17
]
]
A
neighborhood frame
is a pair
(X,N)

consisting of a set
X
and a
neighborhood function

N:X\to\mathfun{PP}X
, where
\mathfun{P}X
is the powerset of
X
.

For
x\in X
, we call elements of
N(x)
the
neighborhoods
of
x
.
A state
x
in
X
then satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi
if the set

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket:=\{y\in X\mid y\Vdash\varphi\}
is a neighborhood of
x
.

Kripke frames can be thought of as special neighborhood frames.
Indeed, if we identify a Kripke frame
(X,R)
with the neighborhood frame

(X,N_{R})
, where
N_{R}(x)=\{b\subseteq X\mid R[x]\subseteq b\}
,
then
the interpretation of
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi
in
(X,R)
and
(X,N_{R})
coincides.
(As usual,
R[x]:=\{y\in X\mid xRy\}
denotes the set of
R
-successors of
x
.)

The algebraic semantics of normal modal logic is given by
modal algebras
.
These are Boolean algebras endowed with a unary function
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt:B\to B
that
preserves finite meets. Together with

they form the category
\mathsf{MA}
.

.
By the Jónsson-Tarski representation theorem
[

]
, each modal algebra

(B,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

can be represented as a subalgebra of the modal algebra

(\mathfun{P}X,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

, where
X
is the set of ultrafilters of
B
and
R
is
defined on
X
by
xRy
iff
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta\in x
implies
a\in y
for each
a\in B
.
In fact, one can endow
X
with a topology
\tau
such that the Boolean
algebra of clopen subsets of
(X,\tau)
is isomorphic to
B
.

This gives rise to the notion of a
descriptive Kripke frame
, that is,
a Kripke frame
(\mathbb{X},R)
where
\mathbb{X}
is a Stone space (a compact
Hausdorff zero-dimensional space) and
R
is a continuous relation on
X
;
see also
[
Ven07
]
.
Let
\mathsf{KF}
be the category of Kripke frames and bounded morphisms and

\mathsf{DKF}
the category of descriptive Kripke frames and continuous
bounded morphisms. We then have the duality for modal algebras and
descriptive frames in Theorem
1
below.
The duality on objects traces back to the 1951 paper by
Jónsson and Tarski
[
JT51
]
and was subsequently
extended to the categorical duality as we know it now
[
Hal56
,
Esa74
,
Gol76
]
.

Theorem 1

(Jónsson-Tarski duality)
.

\mathsf{MA}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{DKF}
.

This theorem generalizes Stone duality
[
Sto36
]
between the category

\mathsf{BA}
of Boolean algebras and Boolean homomorphisms and the category

\mathsf{Stone}
of Stone spaces and continuous functions. To obtain a similar
duality for
\mathsf{KF}
we recall Tarski duality for complete and atomic
Boolean algebras. Let
\mathsf{CABA}
be the category of complete atomic
Boolean algebras and complete Boolean homomorphisms, and let
\mathsf{Set}

be the category of sets and functions. Observe that
\mathsf{CABA}
is a
non-full subcategory of
\mathsf{BA}
.
Then Tarski duality states that
\mathsf{CABA}
is dually equivalent to

\mathsf{Set}
.
On objects, this duality stems from Tarski’s 1935 paper
[
Tar35
]
and
a statement of the full duality can be found in
[
Joh82
, Example 4.6(a)]
.
The name “Tarski duality” was coined recently in
[
BMO19
]
.
The duality is obtained by sending a set
X
to its powerset,
conceived of as a CABA, and a CABA
A
to its set of atoms.
On morphisms, a function
f:X\to X^{\prime}
is sent to its inverse image.
If
h:A\to A^{\prime}
is a complete Boolean homomorphism,
then it has a left adjoint
h^{*}:A^{\prime}\to A
, given by

h^{*}(a^{\prime})=\bigwedge\{a\in A\mid a^{\prime}\leq h(a)\}
,
which restricts to the atoms, and we send
h
to this restriction.
Thus, we obtain the contravariant functors
\wp:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{CABA}
and

\mathfun{at}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{Set}
which yield Tarski duality.

Thomason
[
Tho75
]
generalized Tarski duality to the category
\mathsf{CAMA}
,
whose objects are modal algebras
(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.71 0 C 0.71 0.39 0.39 0.71 0 0.71 C -0.39 0.71 -0.71 0.39 -0.71 0 C -0.71 -0.39 -0.39 -0.71 0 -0.71 C 0.39 -0.71 0.71 -0.39 0.71 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
such that
A
is a CABA
and
is completely multiplicative, i.e., preserves all meets.
The morphisms of
\mathsf{CAMA}
are complete modal algebra homomorphisms.

Theorem 2

(Thomason duality)
.

\mathsf{CAMA}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{KF}
.

2.2.
Duality theory for non-normal modal logics

The algebraic semantics of non-normal classical modal logic is given by pairs

(B,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
where
B
is a Boolean algebra and
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt:B\to B
is an
arbitrary (not necessarily meet-preserving) function. Došen
[
Doš89
]

calls these “modal algebras,” but to avoid confusion we will call them

neighborhood algebras
. We write
\mathsf{NA}
for the category of
neighborhood algebras and
-preserving Boolean homomorphisms.
In addition, we write
\mathsf{NF}
for the category of neighborhood frames and
neighborhood morphisms, where we recall (see, e.g.,

[
Pac17
, Definition 2.9]
) that a
neighborhood morphism

from
(X,N)
to
(X^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a function
f:X\to X^{\prime}
such that

a^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}(f(x))\quad\text{iff}\quad f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\in N(x)

(3)

for all
x\in X
and
a^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
.

Došen
[
Doš89
]
generalized Jónsson-Tarski duality and Thomason
duality to the setting of neighborhood algebras and frames.
The rôle of descriptive Kripke frames is now played by descriptive
neighborhood frames (defined in detail in Section
5.1
).
Together with continuous neighborhood morphisms they comprise the category

\mathsf{DNF}
. Likewise, CAMAs are replaced by
complete atomic neighborhood
algebras
(CANAs), i.e., neighborhood algebras
(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.71 0 C 0.71 0.39 0.39 0.71 0 0.71 C -0.39 0.71 -0.71 0.39 -0.71 0 C -0.71 -0.39 -0.39 -0.71 0 -0.71 C 0.39 -0.71 0.71 -0.39 0.71 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
such that
A
is
complete and atomic. We write
\mathsf{CANA}
for the category of CANAs and
complete neighborhood algebra homomorphisms.

Theorem 3

(Došen
[
Doš89
]
)
.

(1)

\mathsf{NA}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{DNF}
.

(2)

\mathsf{CANA}
is dually equivalent to
\mathsf{NF}
.

2.3.
The algebra/coalgebra approach

Recall
[
AHS90
, Definition 5.37]
that an
algebra
for an endofunctor

\mathfun{L}
on a category
\mathsf{A}
is a pair
(A,\alpha)
consisting of an
object
A\in\mathsf{A}
and a morphism
\alpha:\mathfun{L}A\to A
in
\mathsf{A}
.
An
\mathfun{L}
-algebra morphism
from
(A,\alpha)
to
(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
is
an
\mathsf{A}
-morphism
h:A\to A^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes
in
\mathsf{A}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{L}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{L}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{L}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

We write
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L})
for the category of
\mathfun{L}
-algebras
and
\mathfun{L}
-algebra morphisms.

{exa}

(1)

It is well known that
\mathsf{MA}
is isomorphic to the
category
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})
for the endofunctor

\mathfun{K}:\mathsf{BA}\to\mathsf{BA}
that sends a Boolean algebra
B

to the free Boolean algebra generated by the meet-semilattice underlying

B
(
[
Abr88
]
,
[
Ghi95
, Section 2]
,

[
KKV04
, Proposition 3.17]
).

(2)

As we pointed out in the introduction, free CABAs exist because
CABAs are exactly the Eilenberg-Moore algebras of the double
contravariant powerset monad
[
Tay02
]
(see also

[
BBH+20
, Section 5.1]
), and categories of Eilenberg-Moore
algebras have free objects
[
AHS90
, Proposition 20.7(2)]
.
In
[
BCM22
]
a concrete construction of free CABAs was given and it was
shown that
\mathsf{CAMA}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})
for the
endofunctor
\mathfun{H}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{CABA}
that sends a CABA
A

to the free CABA generated by the complete meet-semilattice underlying
A
.
Explicitly,
\mathfun{H}A
can be described as the free CABA generated
by the set underlying
A
, modulo the axioms stating that
the inclusion map
A\to\mathfun{H}A
preserves all meets.

By an easy adaptation of the proof of
[
Ven07
]
and
[
BCM22
]
,
respectively, one can show that:

Proposition 4
.

(1)

Let
\mathfun{N}:\mathsf{BA}\to\mathsf{BA}
be the composition of the
forgetful functor
\mathsf{BA}\to\mathsf{Set}
and the free functor

\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{BA}
. Then

\mathsf{NA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}).

(2)

Let
\mathfun{L}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{CABA}
be the composition of the
forgetful functor
\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{Set}
and the free functor

\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{CABA}
. Then

\mathsf{CANA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}).

.

B

.
Conversely, an
\mathfun{N}
-algebra
\alpha:\mathfun{N}B\to B
gives rise to
the neighborhood algebra
(B,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

where

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptb)
.
It is easy to verify that these assignments prove the isomorphism of the
first item on objects. The verification on morphisms is a routine exercise.
The second item can be proven analogously.

The dual notion of an algebra is that of a coalgebra.
A
coalgebra
for a functor
\mathfun{T}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}

is a pair
(X,\gamma)
such that
\gamma:X\to\mathfun{T}X
is a morphism
in
\mathsf{C}
. A
\mathfun{T}
-coalgebra morphism
from
(X,\gamma)
to

(X^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
is a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism
f:X\to X^{\prime}
such that the
following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{T}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{T}X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{T}f}

We write
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{T})
for the category of
\mathfun{T}
-coalgebras
and
\mathfun{T}
-coalgebra morphisms.

{exa}

(1)

Let
\mathfun{P}:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
be the covariant
powerset functor on
\mathsf{Set}
. It is well known
(see, e.g.,
[
Rut00
, Example 2.1]
)
that
\mathsf{KF}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{P})
.

(2)

Let
\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
be the contravariant
powerset functor on
\mathsf{Set}
.
1
1

1

Note that
\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is the
composition of
\wp:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{CABA}
with the forgetful
functor
\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{Set}
.

Then
\mathsf{NF}
is isomorphic to

\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}})

[
Ven07
, Example 9.5]
.
We abbreviate
\mathfun{B}:=\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 7.80904pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\textstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 6.16771pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}{\reflectbox{\mbox{\hskip 5.24484pt{1 0 -.55 1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathfun{\mkern 3.5muP\mkern-3.5mu}$}}}}}

so the topology can alternatively be defined by taking the Boolean closure of

\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 0 L 3.57 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta\mid a\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\}
as a basis.)
It is well known that
\mathfun{V}X
is a Stone space, and that the assignment

\mathfun{V}
extends to an endofunctor on
\mathsf{Stone}
by setting

\mathfun{V}f:\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}:c\mapsto f[c]

for a continuous function
f:X\to X^{\prime}
.
It is also well known that
\mathsf{DKF}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})
;
see, e.g.,
[
Esa74
,
Abr88
,
KKV04
]
.

(4)

In Section
5.1
we will define a neighborhood
analogue
\mathfun{D}
of the Vietoris endofunctor such that the category

\mathsf{DNF}
of descriptive neighborhood frames is isomorphic
to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D})
.

Since
\mathsf{MA}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})
and
\mathsf{DKF}
is
isomorphic to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})
, a convenient way to obtain
Jónsson-Tarski duality is to prove that Stone duality between
\mathsf{BA}

and
\mathsf{Stone}
lifts to a dual equivalence between
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{K})

and
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})
. This can be done by lifting Stone duality to a

duality of functors
.

{defi}

We call functors
\mathfun{L}:\mathsf{BA}\to\mathsf{BA}
and

\mathfun{T}:\mathsf{Stone}\to\mathsf{Stone}

Stone-duals
if the diagram
below commutes up to natural isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{BA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Stone}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{BA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Stone}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{uf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{clp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{uf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{clp}}

Here
\mathfun{uf}
and
\mathfun{clp}
are the contravariant functors that
establish Stone duality.
That is,
\mathfun{uf}
denotes the functor that sends a Boolean algebra to
its Stone space of ultrafilters and a homomorphism
h
to
h^{-1}
.
In the other direction,
\mathfun{clp}
is the functor that sends a Stone space to
its Boolean algebra of clopen sets and a continuous function

f
to
f^{-1}
.

Since algebra and coalgebra are dual concepts, we then obtain

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{T}).

Thus, Jónsson-Tarski duality follows from the fact that

\mathfun{K}
and
\mathfun{V}
are Stone-duals
[
KKV04
]
.

Similarly, since
\mathsf{CAMA}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})
and

\mathsf{KF}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{P})
, we can obtain Thomason
duality by lifting Tarski duality between
\mathsf{CABA}
and
\mathsf{Set}
to a
dual equivalence between
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})
and
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{P})
.

{defi}

We call
\mathfun{L}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{CABA}
and

\mathfun{T}:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}

Tarski-duals
if the
diagram below commutes up to natural isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CABA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CABA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{at}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{at}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wp}

As with Stone-dual functors, this gives rise to a duality

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{T}).

Thomason duality can now be obtained from the fact that
\mathfun{H}
and
\mathfun{P}

are Tarski-duals, which is proven in
[
BCM22
, Theorem 4.3]
.

3.
Thomason type dualities for neighborhood frames

In this section we derive Thomason type dualities for classes of neighborhood
frames. We focus on classes of CANAs and classes of neighborhood frames that
are described by the so-called one-step axioms. As we will see, the
corresponding dualities are then obtained as algebra/coalgebra dualities.
Indeed, classes of CANAs defined by one-step axioms can be viewed as
categories of algebras for some endofunctor on
\mathsf{CABA}
, while the
corresponding classes of neighborhood frames as categories of coalgebras for
an endofunctor on
\mathsf{Set}
. We can then obtain the desired duality as a

duality of functors
(see Definition
2.3
).

Our results generalize those in
[
BCM22
]
,
where a functor duality is proved between the endofunctor
\mathfun{H}

on
\mathsf{CABA}
whose algebras are CAMAs (Example
2.3
(
2
))
and the powerset functor
\mathfun{P}:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}

(Example
2.3
(
1
)).
In Example
3.3
we detail how the results of

[
BCM22
]
fit in our general scheme.
The algebra/coalgebra dualities we obtain pave the way for
investigations of the resulting classes of frames using methods of

coalgebraic logic
, such as developed in e.g.
[
Ven07
,
KP11
]
.

As running examples, we derive Thomason duality for Kripke frames
[
Tho75
]

and Došen duality for neighborhood frames
[
Doš89
, Theorem 12]
.
Section
4
is dedicated to deriving new dualities
for various classes of neighborhood frames using
the theory from this section.

3.1.
Infinitary languages

We will work in a modal language with arbitrary conjunctions.
Throughout the paper, for each cardinal
\kappa
we
fix a set of variables
V_{\kappa}
of cardinality
\kappa
such that

\lambda\leq\kappa
implies
V_{\lambda}\subseteq V_{\kappa}

for all cardinals
\lambda
,
\kappa
.
Define
\mathfrak{L}^{\kappa}
to be the set of formulae generated by the grammar

\textstyle\varphi::=v\mid\top\mid\neg\varphi\mid\bigwedge_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}

where
v\in V_{\kappa}
and
I
is some index set of cardinality
<\kappa
.
We then define
\mathfrak{L}
as the proper class that contains the formulae in

\mathfrak{L}^{\kappa}
for all cardinals
\kappa
.

The study of propositional and first-order languages with infinite
conjunctions was pioneered by Scott and Tarski
[
ST58
]
and
Tarski
[
Tar58
]
. In both these references
the size of conjunctions (and quantifiers) is bounded by some cardinal.
First-order logics that allow conjunctions and disjunctions over arbitrary
sets of formulae have been studied comprehensively; see, e.g.,
[
Cha68
]
,

[
BF85
, Part C]
,
[
Bel16
]
, and the references therein.

We next extend the grammar of
\mathfrak{L}
with a modal operator.

{defi}

For each cardinal
\kappa
,
define
\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}^{\kappa}

generated by the grammar

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi

where

\kappa
.

as axioms.

An

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
is a family
\vartheta_{\kappa}:V_{\kappa}\to A
,
where
\kappa
ranges over the cardinals,
such that
\vartheta_{\lambda}
and
\vartheta_{\kappa}
agree on all variables in
V_{\lambda}
whenever
\lambda\leq\kappa
.
Every assignment

.

We sometimes write
\vartheta:V\to A
for the family of assignments

\vartheta_{\kappa}:V_{\kappa}\to A
. By
v\in V
we then mean a variable
in
V_{\kappa}
for some
\kappa
, and
\vartheta(v)
denotes
\vartheta_{\kappa}(v)
,
where
\kappa
is such that
v\in V_{\kappa}
.
An assignment
\vartheta:V\to A
for a CABA
A

gives rise to several other maps, listed in Table
1
.
Note that we use the font
\mathfrak{L}
for languages, and

\mathfun{L}
for the endofunctor on
\mathsf{CABA}
from Proposition
4
.

Def.
3.3

Table 1.
Different maps arising from
\vartheta
.

{defi}

If
\Ax
is a class of axioms, then we write
\mathsf{CANA}(\Ax)
for the
full subcategory of
\mathsf{CANA}
whose objects validate
\Ax
.

3.2.
One-step axioms

We next concentrate on the so-called one-step axioms. Intuitively, these are
formulae in
\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}
such that every variable occurs in the scope of
precisely one box. We will see that, in case
\Ax
consists solely of one-step
axioms, the category
\mathsf{CANA}(\Ax)
is isomorphic to

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}_{\Ax})
for some endofunctor
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}
on
\mathsf{CABA}
.

,

as the set of
formulae generated by the grammar

A

is a formula

.

Remark 5
.

When viewed as formulae in the modal language
\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}
, the formulae in

(\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt})^{1}
are sometimes referred to as “formulae of modal depth 1”
or “rank 1 formulae.” This justifies our notation. Observe that every
one-step axiom is in particular an axiom in the sense of Section
3.1
.

are not one-step axioms.

The appeal of one-step axioms lies in the fact that they define endofunctors
on
\mathsf{CABA}
in a structured way. All of these are subfunctors of

\mathfun{L}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{CABA}

from Proposition
4
(
2
).
If
\varphi
is a one-step axiom and

\vartheta:V\to A
is an assignment, then
\vartheta
gives rise to a map

\overline{\vartheta}:(\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt})^{1}\to\mathfun{L}A
.
Note that this does
not
rely on any CANA-structure on
A
.
Indeed, we can use the fact that
\varphi
is of modal depth 1 and define

\overline{\vartheta}
as follows.

{defi}

For a CABA
A
and assignment
\vartheta:V\to A

we define
\overline{\vartheta}:(\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

recursively via

\displaystyle\overline{\vartheta}(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\pi)

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\widehat{\vartheta}(\pi),

for
\pi\in\mathfrak{L}
(which is well defined because
\pi

does not contain any boxes), and

\displaystyle\overline{\vartheta}(\top)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\overline{\vartheta}(\neg\varphi)

\displaystyle=\neg\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\overline{\vartheta}\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{i\in I}\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi_{i})

Intuitively, the endofunctor on
\mathsf{CABA}
corresponding to a collection

\Ax
of one-step axioms sends
A\in\mathsf{CABA}
to the free complete atomic Boolean
algebra generated by
A
modulo (instantiations of) the axioms,
i.e., modulo the relations
\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi)=1
, where
\varphi\in\Ax

and
\vartheta
is an assignment for
A
.
Before defining this functor, we recall the notion of a
complete congruence on a CABA.

{defi}

A
complete congruence
on a CABA
A
is an equivalence relation
\sim

on the underlying set such that
a\sim b
implies
\neg a\sim\neg b
,
and
a_{i}\sim b_{i}
for all
i
in some index set
I
implies

\bigwedge_{i\in I}a_{i}\sim\bigwedge_{i\in I}b_{i}
.

If
\sim
is a complete congruence on
A
, then
we can define the quotient CABA
A/{\sim}
. Writing
[a]
for the
equivalence class of
a
, the CABA-operations on
A/{\sim}
are
defined by
1=[1_{A}]
,
\neg[a]=[\neg a]
,
and
\bigwedge[a_{i}]=[\bigwedge a_{i}]
.
Furthermore, the quotient map
q:A\to A/{\sim}:a\mapsto[a]

is a complete homomorphism.

The collection of complete congruences on a CABA is closed under arbitrary
intersections. Therefore, if we have a collection
R
of equations on
A
(that is, a collection of equations of the form
a=b
, where
a,b\in A
),
then we can define the complete congruence generated by
R
to be the smallest
complete congruence
\sim_{R}
that contains
a\sim_{R}b
for all equations

a=b
in
R
.
Consequently,
A/{\sim_{R}}
is the largest quotient (= complete homomorphic image)
of
A
in which all equations in
R
hold.

{defi}

Let
\Ax
be a class of one-step axioms. Write

\Ax^{\kappa}:=\Ax\cap\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}^{\kappa}
for the subset
of one-step axioms from
\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}^{\kappa}

and
\Ax^{\leq\kappa}
for the union
\bigcup_{\lambda\leq\kappa}\Ax^{\lambda}
.
We call
\Ax

increasing
if for every CABA
A

and assignment
\vartheta:V\to A
, the following condition holds:
If
\kappa
is the cardinality of the powerset of
\mathfun{L}A

and
\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi)=1
for all
\varphi\in\Ax^{\leq\kappa}
,
then
\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi)=1
for all
\varphi\in\Ax
.

The idea behind an increasing set of axioms is that, when we want to
take the quotient of a free CABA with axioms in a proper class
\Ax
,
we only need to use axioms from
\mathfrak{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.5 -2.5 M -2.5 -2.46 L -2.5 2.46 C -2.5 2.48 -2.48 2.5 -2.46 2.5 L 2.46 2.5 C 2.48 2.5 2.5 2.48 2.5 2.46 L 2.5 -2.46 C 2.5 -2.48 2.48 -2.5 2.46 -2.5 L -2.46 -2.5 C -2.48 -2.5 -2.5 -2.48 -2.5 -2.46 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}^{\kappa}
,
where
\kappa
is the cardinality of the powerset of the free CABA.
This ensures that the quotient is well defined.
An example of a class of axioms that is increasing
is given after Example
3.2
.

If
\Ax
consists of a set of axioms (rather than a proper class),
then we can make it increasing by simply adding for each axiom

\varphi\in\Ax^{\kappa}
, all instantiations
of
\varphi
where the variables are replaced by variables from

V_{\lambda}
where
\lambda<\kappa
.
In practice, when dealing with a set of axioms, we will not make this
explicit.

We are now ready to define an endofunctor
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}
on
\mathsf{CABA}

from an increasing collection
\Ax
of one-step axioms.

{defi}

Let
\Ax
be an increasing collection of one-step axioms.
For
A\in\mathsf{CABA}
, define
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
to be
the free complete atomic Boolean algebra generated by the set

\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

modulo the congruence

generated by

\{\overline{\vartheta}(\varphi)\sim_{\Ax}1\}
, where

for the variables in

.
For a complete homomorphism

on generators by

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta)=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

If
\Ax
consists of a single axiom
\mathsf{ax}
, then we write

\mathfun{L}_{\mathsf{(ax)}}
instead of
\mathfun{L}_{\{\mathsf{(ax)}\}}
.
Many well-known functors in modal logic can be obtained via
Definition
3.2
. We give two examples.

{exa}

If we work with no axioms (i.e.,
\Ax=\varnothing)
, then the
functor that arises from Definition
3.2
is precisely the
functor
\mathfun{L}
from Proposition
4
(
2
).

In our next example we recover the endofunctor

\mathfun{H}:\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{CABA}
from
[
BCM22
, Section 4]
,
where

sends a CABA

to the free CABA generated by the

(see Example
2.3
(

modulo

\bigwedge_{b\in X}\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

for every

.
Although this looks like the instantiation of a single one-step axiom, it is not.
Indeed, if we define

\varphi=\bigwedge_{v\in V_{\kappa}}\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\bigg(\bigwedge V_{\kappa}\bigg)

then the axiom
\varphi
only implies
\kappa
-distributivity
.
To remedy this, we work with a class of axioms indexed by the class
\Card

of cardinal numbers. For each
\kappa\in\Card
, define

\mathsf{(C_{\kappa})}

\bigwedge\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\bigwedge V_{\kappa}
.

Now set

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}=\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:Ckap}{$\mathsf{(C_{\kappa})}$}}\mid\kappa\in\Card\}.

{exa}

Consider the increasing collection of axioms
\Ax=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.
Then the construction of Definition
3.2
yields the
functor
\mathfun{H}
from
[
BCM22
]
.
Its algebras correspond to CAMAs.

Thus, incidentally, the previous example also illustrates the need to allow
a proper class of axioms, rather than just a set.

is
well defined.

It is easy to see that the two assignments above define a bijection between
objects of
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}_{\Ax})
and objects of
\mathsf{CANA}(\Ax)
.
We can extend this to a natural isomorphism in a standard way.

Theorem 6
.

If
\Ax
is an increasing collection of one-step axioms, then

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}_{\Ax})\cong\mathsf{CANA}(\Ax).

Proof 3.1

(Proof sketch.)
.

The isomorphism on objects has already been sketched.
To prove the isomorphism on morphisms, let
(A,\alpha)
and

(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
be two
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}
-algebras with the corresponding
CANAs
(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

-algebra

In other words, (
4
) holds iff

a CANA-morphism.

Going back to the class of axioms
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
from Example
3.2
, we see that Theorem
6

generalizes
[
BCM22
, Theorem 4.7]
:

{exa}

Suppose
\Ax=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
.
Then
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}=\mathfun{H}
and
\mathsf{CANA}(\Ax)\cong\mathsf{CAMA}
,
so that

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{H})\cong\mathsf{CAMA}.

3.3.
A functor duality theorem

We now classify the atoms of

\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
as subsets of
A
. This then gives rise to an endofunctor on

\mathsf{Set}
which is a subfunctor of
\mathfun{B}
of
Example
2.3
(
2
) and is dual to
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}
.
We make use of the following notation to characterize the subsets of
A
we are
interested in.

{defi}

Let
A
be a set and
a\in A
. Define

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta=\{W\subseteq A\mid a\in W\}.

We can assign to each one-step axiom a subset of
\mathfun{P}A
.

{defi}

Let
\varphi\in(\mathfrak{L}^{\kappa}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.92pt{\vbox to3.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.95903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt})^{1}
be a one-step axiom,

A
a set and
\vartheta:V\to A
an
assignment.
Define
\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)
to be
the subset of
\mathfun{P}A
given recursively by:

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptv)

\displaystyle=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 0 L 3.57 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\vartheta(v)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\top)

\displaystyle=\mathfun{P}A

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\neg\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathfun{P}A\setminus\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}\textstyle(\bigwedge\varphi_{i})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\bigcap\{\vartheta^{t}(\varphi_{i})\}

It then follows that:

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}({\textstyle\bigvee}\varphi_{i})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\vartheta^{t}(\varphi_{i})\}

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\varphi\to\psi)

\displaystyle=\{W\subseteq A\mid W\in\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)\Rightarrow W\in\vartheta^{t}(\psi)\}

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle=\{W\subseteq A\mid W\in\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)\Leftrightarrow W\in\vartheta^{t}(\psi)\}

We say that
W
is a
\varphi
-subset
of
A

if
W\in\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)
for every assignment
\vartheta
of the variables in
V
.
If
\Ax
is a collection of axioms, then we say that

W
is an
\Ax
-subset
if
W
is a
\varphi
-subset for all
\varphi\in\Ax
.

The next lemma witnesses the significance of
\Ax
-subsets by
proving a bijective correspondence between atoms
of
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
and
\Ax
-subsets of
A
.
Recall that atoms of a CABA
A
correspond bijectively to complete
homomorphisms into the two-element Boolean algebra
2
: If
a\in A
is an atom, then

p_{a}:A\to 2
, given by
p_{a}(b)=1
iff
a\leq b
, defines a
complete homomorphism. Conversely, every complete homomorphism
p
arises in this way,
where
a=\bigwedge\{b\in A\mid p(b)=1\}
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
A\in\mathsf{CABA}
and let
\Ax
be an increasing collection of axioms.
Then the atoms of
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
correspond bijectively
to
\Ax
-subsets of
A
.

Proof 3.2
.

We view atoms of
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
as complete homomorphisms

p:\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A\to 2
.
Since
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
is defined by generators and relations,

p
is uniquely determined by its action
on the generators of
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}A
, i.e., the elements of the
form

with
