\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\top)

\displaystyle=\mathfun{P}B

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\neg\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathfun{P}B\setminus\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{t}(\varphi\wedge\psi)

\displaystyle=\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)\cap\vartheta^{t}(\psi)

Let
\varphi
be a finitary one-step axiom.
Then we call
W\in\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
a
\varphi
-subset of
B
if
W\in\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)

for every assignment
\vartheta
of the variables
V_{\omega}
.
If
\Ax
is a collection of finitary one-step axioms, then we say that

W
is an
\Ax
-subset
if
W
is a
\varphi
-subset
for all axioms
\varphi\in\Ax
.

The next lemma is an analogue of Lemma
7
.

Lemma 27
.

Ultrafilters of
\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}B
correspond bijectively to
\Ax
-subsets of
B
.

Proof 5.3
.

The bijection is established by sending an ultrafilter of
\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}B

(viewed as a homomorphism
p:\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}B\to 2
) to the set

W_{p}=\{b\in B\mid p(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptb)=1\}\subseteq B,

(9)

and by sending any
\Ax
-subset
W\subseteq B
to the map

p_{W}:\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}B\to 2
defined on generators by
p_{W}(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptb)=1
iff
b\in W
.
The remainder of the proof can be obtained from the proof of Lemma
7
by
replacing “
\mathfun{L}
” with
``\mathfun{N}
”,
“
\mathfun{L}_{\Ax}
” with “
\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}
”,
“complete homomorphism” with “homomorphism”,
and “complete congruence” with “congruence”.

We can now define the functor
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}
in a similar manner to

\mathfun{B}_{\Ax}
from Definition
3.3
.

{defi}

For a Stone space
\mathbb{X}
, let
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
be the subspace
of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
whose objects are
\Ax
-subsets of
\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
.
For a continuous function
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
let

\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}f
be the restriction of
\mathfun{D}f
to
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
.

While for any set
X
the set
\mathfun{B}_{\Ax}X
is automatically a subset
of
\mathfun{B}X
, in our current setting it is not guaranteed that

\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
is a Stone subspace of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
.
The next proposition ensures that this is indeed the case.

Proposition 28
.

\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}
is an endofunctor on
\mathsf{Stone}
.

Proof 5.4
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Stone space. In order to show that
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}

is again a Stone space it suffices to prove that it is a closed subspace
of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
.
Note that
\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)
is a clopen subset of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
for any
finitary one-step axiom
\varphi
and assignment
\vartheta:V_{\omega}\to\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
.
By definition, the set underlying
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
is given by

\bigcap\{\vartheta^{t}(\varphi)\mid\varphi\in\Ax,\vartheta:V_{\omega}\to\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\}\subseteq\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}.

(10)

Since this is the intersection of clopen subsets of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
,
it is a closed subset of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
.

That
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}
is well defined on morphisms can be proven as in
Proposition
9
, and functoriality of
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}

follows from functoriality of
\mathfun{D}
.

The next theorem is an analogue of Theorem
10
in the setting
of descriptive frames.

Theorem 29

(Stone Functor Duality Theorem)
.

Let
\Ax
be a collection of finitary one-step axioms.
Then the functors
\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}
and
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}
are Stone-duals.

Proof 5.5
.

We view ultrafilters as homomorphisms, so for example when we say

p\in\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}))
we view

p
as a homomorphism
\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X})\to 2
.
For a Stone space
\mathbb{X}
, define the function

\xi_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}))\to\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}

by
p\mapsto W_{p}
(defined as in (
9
)).
This yields a bijection on objects by Lemma
27
.

To see that
\xi_{\mathbb{X}}
is continuous,
recall that the topology on
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
is generated by

.
Since
\xi_{\mathbb{X}}
is a continuous bijection between Stone spaces, it
is a homeomorphism, i.e., an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Stone}
.

Finally, we prove that the assignment

\xi=(\xi_{\mathbb{X}})_{\mathbb{X}\in\mathsf{Stone}}:\mathfun{uf}\circ\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}\circ\mathfun{clp}\to\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}

is natural by showing that
for every continuous function
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\mathbb{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}

commutes.
To this end, let
p\in\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}))

and
a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
.
Then

\displaystyle a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}f\circ\xi_{\mathbb{X}}(p)

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\xi_{\mathbb{X}}(p)(f^{-1}(a^{\prime}))=1

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad p(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptf^{-1}(a^{\prime}))=1

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad p(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}f)(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta^{\prime}))=1

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}f))(p)(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta^{\prime})=1

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad a^{\prime}\in\xi_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}\circ\mathfun{uf}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}(\mathfun{clp}f))(p).

This proves the theorem.

Corollary 30
.

For every set
\Ax
of finitary one-step axioms, we have

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}).

Some well-known dualities are instantiations of Corollary
30
.

{exa}

If we take
\Ax=\varnothing
, then we recover Došen’s duality for descriptive
neighborhood frames
[
Doš89
, Theorem 6]
:

\mathsf{NA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D})\cong\mathsf{DNF}.

{exa}

We can derive Jónsson-Tarski duality for normal modal algebras
as follows. As we pointed out in Remark
26
,

\mathfun{V}=\mathfun{D}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:N}{$\mathsf{(N)}$}},\text{\hyperlink{ax:C}{$\mathsf{(C)}$}}\}}
.
Moreover, it is immediate from the definition of
\mathfun{N}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:N}{$\mathsf{(N)}$}},\text{\hyperlink{ax:C}{$\mathsf{(C)}$}}\}}

that
\mathsf{MA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:N}{$\mathsf{(N)}$}},\text{\hyperlink{ax:C}{$\mathsf{(C)}$}}\}})
.
Therefore,

\mathsf{MA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:N}{$\mathsf{(N)}$}},\text{\hyperlink{ax:C}{$\mathsf{(C)}$}}\}})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:N}{$\mathsf{(N)}$}},\text{\hyperlink{ax:C}{$\mathsf{(C)}$}}\}})\cong\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{V})\cong\mathsf{DKF}.

Corollary
30
also gives rise to the notion of
a descriptive contingency frame, as shown in the next example.

{exa}

Since
\mathsf{(Cont)}
is a finitary axiom, it gives rise to the notions of contingency neighborhood algebras,
descriptive contingency neighborhood frames, and a duality between them.
A
contingency neighborhood algebra
is a neighborhood
algebra

such that

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptb=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\neg b
. We write

\mathsf{CNA}
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{NA}
of contingency neighborhood algebras.
A
descriptive contingency neighborhood frame
is a
\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:cont}{$\mathsf{(Cont)}$}}}
-coalgebra.
Explicitly we can describe these as tuples
(X,N,A)
such that

(X,N,A)
is a descriptive neighborhood frame and

(X,N)
is a contingency neighborhood frame.
Writing
\mathsf{DCNF}
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{DNF}

whose objects are descriptive contingency neighborhood frames,
we obtain:

\mathsf{CNA}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:cont}{$\mathsf{(Cont)}$}}})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:cont}{$\mathsf{(Cont)}$}}})\cong\mathsf{DCNF}.

5.3.
Forgetting incorrectly

Just like
\mathsf{(Cont)}
in Example
5.2
,
the axiom
\mathsf{(M)}
is also finitary. Therefore, we get functors

\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
and
\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
(shorthand for
\mathfun{N}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}\}}
and

\mathfun{D}_{\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}\}}
) such that

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})
.
Moreover, we have

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\cong\mathsf{BAM}.

Thus, one may think that the coalgebras in
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})

give a suitable notion of descriptive monotone frames.
However, care is needed:
if we take a
\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
-coalgebra and
forget about the topology, we do
not
obtain a
\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
-coalgebra.
Indeed, since only clopen sets are allowed to serve as neighborhoods
of a state, the collection of neighborhoods at a state need not be
upward closed under inclusion.
We give an example of this phenomenon.

{exa}

Let
X=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and generate a topology
\tau
on
X
by
the finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
and cofinite sets containing
\infty
. Thus,
\mathbb{X}=(X,\tau)
is the one-point compactification of the discrete space
\mathbb{N}
.
Clearly
\mathbb{X}
is a Stone space.
Define
\gamma:\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}\mathbb{X}
by

\gamma(x)=\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}.

Then
(\mathbb{X},\gamma)
is a
\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
-coalgebra.
However,
(X,\gamma)
does not define a
\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
-coalgebra
because
\mathbb{N}_{\odd}=\{x\in\mathbb{N}\mid x\text{ is odd}\}

is not clopen, hence it is not in
\gamma(x)
, while both
\varnothing\in\gamma(x)

and
\varnothing\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\odd}
.

A similar problem occurs with descriptive convex frames.
In fact, Example
5.3
also shows that
if we forget about the topological structure of a
\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:conv}{$\mathsf{(Conv)}$}}}
-coalgebra,
then we do not necessarily end up with a
\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:conv}{$\mathsf{(Conv)}$}}}
-coalgebra
because
\varnothing\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\odd}\subseteq X

and
\varnothing,X\in\gamma(x)
.
One way to remedy this is by looking at canonical extensions.
Such an approach was carried out in
[
Han03
]
for the special case of
monotone frames.
We explore this idea in the next section.

6.
Canonical extensions

In this section we discuss the
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions of (descriptive)
neighborhood algebras. In general, these extensions are not functorial.
However, we show that adding the convexity and co-convexity axioms
yields functoriality of the
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions, respectively.
We also show that adding these axioms allows us to view descriptive
frames as categories of coalgebras.
We use this to give an alternative coalgebraic proof of the duality for
monotone Boolean algebra expansions of
[
HK04
]
.

6.1.
\sigma
- and
\pi
-exensions

There is an obvious forgetful functor
\mathsf{DNF}\to\mathsf{NF}
.
Although this forgetful functor is conveniently simple, it has an undesirable
property: it “leaves gaps.” For example, if
(X,N,A)
is a monotone
descriptive frame, then
(X,N)
is
not
generally a monotone frame.
Indeed, monotonicity now only holds with respect to admissible sets (clopens).
Similarly, if
(X,N,A)
is a normal descriptive frame and
R_{N}
is the
relation that arises from
N
via
R_{N}(x)=\bigcap N(x)
, then we would prefer
that the underlying neighborhood frame be normal as well.
This would be the case if
(X,N)
satisfied
a\in N(x)
iff

R_{N}[x]\subseteq a
for each
a\subseteq X
.
Again, since
N(x)
contains only admissible subsets
of
X
, this need not be the case.
2
2

2

For example, let
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
be the
one-point compactification of the discrete space
\mathbb{N}
and
X

the set underlying
\mathbb{X}
. For each
x\in X
let
N(x)
be the
collection of co-finite subsets of
X
containing
\infty
. Then it is easy
to see that
(X,N,\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X})
is a descriptive neighborhood frame,
that
R_{N}(x)=\{\infty\}
, but that
\{\infty\}\notin N(x)
.

To remedy this, we explore alternatives to the forgetful functor.
These are dual versions of the well-known
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions
from the theory of canonical extensions
(see, e.g.,
[
JT51
,
GJ94
,
GH01
,
GJ04
]
):

{defi}

Let
A
be a CABA and
B
a Boolean subalgebra of
A
.

(1)

B
is called
dense
in
A
if every element in
A
is the
join of meets of elements in
B
.

(2)

We say that
B
is
compact
in
A
if for all sets

S,T\subseteq B
with
\bigwedge S\leq\bigvee T
in
A
,
there exist finite
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
such that

\bigwedge S^{\prime}\leq\bigvee T^{\prime}
.

(3)

A
canonical extension
of a Boolean algebra
B
is a pair

(B^{\sigma},e)
where
B^{\sigma}
is a CABA and

e:B\to B^{\sigma}
is a Boolean embedding such that
e[B]
is
dense and compact in
B^{\sigma}
.

Remark 31
.

It is well known
[
JT51
]

that the canonical extension of a Boolean algebra is unique up to isomorphism,
and can explicitly be described as the powerset of the dual Stone space of
B
.
Thus, we often speak of
B^{\sigma}
as
the
canonical extension of
B

and view
B
as sitting inside
B^{\sigma}
.

Let
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.71 0 C 0.71 0.39 0.39 0.71 0 0.71 C -0.39 0.71 -0.71 0.39 -0.71 0 C -0.71 -0.39 -0.39 -0.71 0 -0.71 C 0.39 -0.71 0.71 -0.39 0.71 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

be an endofunction (not necessarily a homomorphism).
Then we can extend
to a function

B^{\sigma}\to B^{\sigma}
in several ways.
Two well-known extensions are the
\sigma
- and
\pi
-extensions.
To define these, we recall the notions of closed and open elements of

B^{\sigma}

[
JT51
, Definition 1.20]
.

We say that
x\in B^{\sigma}
is
closed
if it is a meet
of elements from
B
, and
x
is
open
if it is a join of elements from
B
.
We write
\mathfun{K}_{B}
and
\mathfun{O}_{B}
for the sets of closed and
open elements of
B^{\sigma}
, respectively.

- and

These give maps
\sigma,\pi:\mathsf{NA}\to\mathsf{CANA}
, which in turn give rise to

\Sigma,\Pi:\mathsf{DNF}\to\mathsf{NF}
by composing as follows:

\Sigma=\mathfun{at}\circ\sigma\circ\mathfun{clp}\quad\text{and}\quad\Pi=\mathfun{at}\circ\pi\circ\mathfun{clp}.

Thus we have the following diagram:

(11)

We point out that
\sigma,\pi:\mathsf{NA}\to\mathsf{CANA}

are not necessarily functors (see
[
BMM08
, Example 3.4 and Remark 3.5]
),
and hence neither are
\Sigma
and
\Pi
.
We will temporarily ignore this issue and focus solely on the action of

\sigma
and
\pi
on objects.

We next define
\Sigma
and
\Pi
explicitly.
For this we need the notions of closed and open elements of
(X,N,A)
.
These are defined to be the closed and open sets of the topological space

\mathbb{X}=(X,\tau_{A})
, and denoted by
\mathfun{K}_{A}
and
\mathfun{O}_{A}
, respectively.
Finally, for
c,d\in\mathfun{P}X
define

[c,d]=\{e\in\mathfun{P}X\mid c\subseteq e\subseteq d\}
.

{defi}

Let
(X,N,A)
be a descriptive neighborhood frame.

(1)

Define the
\sigma
-extension of
N
by

N^{\sigma}(x)=\{e\in\mathfun{P}X\mid\exists c\in\mathfun{K}_{A},d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\subseteq d\text{ and }[c,d]\cap A\subseteq N(x)\},

and set
\Sigma(X,N,A)=(X,N^{\sigma})
.

(2)

Define the
\pi
-extension of
N
by

N^{\pi}(x)=\{e\in\mathfun{P}X\mid\forall c\in\mathfun{K}_{A},d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\subseteq d\text{ we have }[c,d]\cap A\cap N(x)\neq\varnothing\},

and set
\Pi(X,N,A)=(X,N^{\pi})
.

Proposition 32
.

The following diagrams commute on objects, up to natural isomorphism.

.
We aim to show that for all

and

we have

iff

.
By definition,

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt_{N}a\mid a\in A,c\subseteq a\subseteq d\}\mid c\in\mathfun{K}_{A},d\in\mathfun{O}_{A},c\subseteq e\subseteq d\Big\}.

Thus,
e\in N^{\prime}(x)
iff
x\in\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt_{N}^{\sigma}e
, which happens iff there are closed
c

and open
d
such that
c\subseteq e\subseteq d
and

a\in[c,d]\cap A
implies
x\in\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0.71 0 C 0.71 0.39 0.39 0.71 0 0.71 C -0.39 0.71 -0.71 0.39 -0.71 0 C -0.71 -0.39 -0.39 -0.71 0 -0.71 C 0.39 -0.71 0.71 -0.39 0.71 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt_{N}a
.
The latter means that we have
a\in N(x)
for all such
a
, and hence

c
and
d
witness the fact that
e\in N^{\sigma}(x)
.

A similar reasoning proves the statement for
\pi
-extensions.

The extensions
\Sigma
and
\Pi
are closely related.
To see this, we need the notion of
dual
(descriptive) neighborhood frames.

{defi}

For a neighborhood frame
(X,N)
, define

N^{c}:X\to\mathfun{PP}X:x\mapsto\{a\subseteq X\mid a\notin N(x)\}.

We call
(X,N)^{c}:=(X,N^{c})
the
complement
of
(X,N)
.

If
(X,N,A)
is a descriptive neighborhood frame,
then we define

N^{c}_{A}:X\to\mathfun{PP}X:x\mapsto\{a\in A\mid a\notin N(x)\}.

It is easy to see that
(X,N_{A}^{c},A)
is again a descriptive frame.
We call
(X,N_{A}^{c},A)
the
complement
of
(X,N,A)

and denote it by
(X,N,A)^{c}
.

It is easy to see that
((X,N)^{c})^{c}=(X,N)
. Moreover,

(\cdot)^{c}:\mathsf{NF}\to\mathsf{NF}
defines an involution,
where
f^{c}=f
for a neighborhood morphism
f
.
Similar statements hold for descriptive frames and the descriptive complement.

Proposition 33
.

Let
(X,N,A)
be a descriptive neighborhood frame.
Then

\Pi(X,N,A)=(\Sigma(X,N^{c}_{A},A))^{c}.

Consequently,
\Sigma(X,N,A)=(\Pi(X,N^{c}_{A},A))^{c}
.

Proof 6.2
.

Note that
(\Sigma(X,N^{c}_{A},A))^{c}=(X,((N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma})^{c})
.
We need to prove that for all
x\in X
and
e\subseteq X
we have

e\in N^{\pi}(x)\quad\text{iff}\quad e\in((N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma})^{c}(x).

(12)

We do so by unravelling the definitions.

For
x\in X
and
e\subseteq X
we have

e\in((N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma})^{c}(x)
iff
e\notin(N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma}(x)
.
In other words,
e\in((N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma})^{c}(x)
iff
we can find no closed
c\in\mathfun{K}_{A}
and open
d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}

such that
c\subseteq e\subseteq d
and
[c,d]\cap A\subseteq N^{c}(x)
.
Therefore,
e\in((N^{c}_{A})^{\sigma})^{c}(x)
iff
for all
c\in\mathfun{K}_{A}
and
d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}
such that

c\subseteq e\subseteq d
we have
[c,d]\cap A\cap N(x)\neq\varnothing
.
But this is exactly the definition of
e\in N^{\pi}(x)
, so
(
12
) holds.

6.2.
\sigma
- and
\pi
-descriptive frames

As we have seen in Section
5.3
,
we do not always have a forgetful functor

\mathfun{U}:\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})
.
In particular, recall that convexity and monotonicity are not preserved.
This is, in part, solved by replacing
\mathfun{U}
with either

\Sigma
or
\Pi
.

If
(X,N,A)
is monotone, then the definition of
N^{\sigma}
simplifies to

N^{\sigma}(x)=\{e\in\mathfun{P}X\mid\exists c\in\mathfun{K}_{A}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\text{ and }[c,X]\cap A\subseteq N(x)\}.

It is then easy to see that the neighborhood frame

\Sigma(X,N,A)=(X,N^{\sigma})
is monotone as well.
Next suppose
(X,N,A)
is convex and there are
e,e^{\prime}\in N^{\sigma}(x)

and
e^{\prime\prime}\subseteq X
such that
e\subseteq e^{\prime\prime}\subseteq e^{\prime}
.
Then by definition of
N^{\sigma}
we have closed sets
c,c^{\prime}
and open sets

d,d^{\prime}
such that
e\in[c,d]
,
e^{\prime}\in[c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]
,
[c,d]\cap A\subseteq N(x)

and
[c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]\cap A\subseteq N(x)
.
Since
c\subseteq e\subseteq e^{\prime\prime}\subseteq e^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}
,
convexity of
(X,N,A)
implies
[c,d^{\prime}]\cap A\subseteq N(x)
.
This, in turn, witnesses the fact that
e^{\prime\prime}\in N^{\sigma}(x)
.
Therefore,
(X,N^{\sigma})
is a convex neighborhood frame.

Thus, on objects we have the following well-defined assignments:

\Sigma:\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Sigma:\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:conv}{$\mathsf{(Conv)}$}}})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:conv}{$\mathsf{(Conv)}$}}}).

From the connection between
\Sigma
and
\Pi
discussed in
Proposition
33
we get that

\Pi:\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Pi:\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:coconv}{$\mathsf{(CoConv)}$}}})\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:coconv}{$\mathsf{(CoConv)}$}}})

are well-defined assignments as well. Here
\mathsf{(CoConv)}
denotes the co-convexity axiom

\mathsf{(CoConv)}

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptv\to\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt(v\wedge v^{\prime})\vee\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt(v\vee v^{\prime\prime})

On neighborhood frames
(X,N)
this corresponds to
N^{c}(x)
being convex for all
x\in X
.

We can turn the assignments
\Sigma
and
\Pi
into “proper” forgetful functors
by incorporating the additional neighborhoods that arise from

\Sigma
or
\Pi
into the notion of a descriptive frame.
This yields two alternative definitions of descriptive frames:

\sigma
-descriptive and
\pi
-descriptive frames.
In what follows we will focus on
\sigma
-descriptive frames,
leaving the dual treatment of
\pi
-descriptive frames to the reader.

{defi}

A
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frame
is a general neighborhood frame

(X,N,A)
that is differentiated and compact (see Definition
5.1
)
and satisfies the following modification of the tightness condition:
for all
x\in X
and
e\in\mathfun{P}X

e\in N(x)\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists c\in\mathfun{K}_{A},d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\subseteq d\text{ and }[c,d]\cap A\subseteq N(x).

We write
\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}
for the category of
\sigma
-descriptive
neighborhood frames and general neighborhood morphisms.

If
(X,N,A)
is a descriptive neighborhood frame, then

(X,N^{\sigma},A)
is a
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frame.
Conversely, given a
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frame
(X,N,A)
,
setting
N_{A}(x)=N(x)\cap A
yields a descriptive neighborhood
frame
(X,N_{A},A)
. It is straightforward to see that these two assignments
give rise to a bijective correspondence between objects in
\mathsf{DNF}
and

\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}
.
Thus, the following diagram commutes on objects, where
\mathfun{U}
is the
forgetful functor that does not add any neighborhoods:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DNF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{NF}}
1-1
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}}

While in descriptive neighborhood frames (Definition
5.1
)
the tightness condition stipulates
that all neighborhoods are admissible sets,
\sigma
-tightness allows non-admissible sets
to act as neighborhoods too.

Remark 34
.

Let
\mathsf{DNF}^{\pi}
be the category of
\pi
-descriptive frames,
defined analogously to Definition
6.2
.
That is, a
\pi
-descriptive frame is a general neighborhood frame that is
differentiated and compact and satisfies for all
x\in X
and
e\subseteq X
:

e\in N(x)\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall c\in\mathfun{K}_{A},d\in\mathfun{O}_{A}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\subseteq d\text{ we have }[c,d]\cap A\cap N(x)\neq\varnothing.

We have that
(X,N,A)
is
\sigma
-descriptive iff
(X,N^{c}_{A},A)
is

\pi
-descriptive.
Moreover,

f:(X,N,A)\to(X^{\prime},N^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is a
general morphism between
\sigma
-descriptive frames iff

f
is a general morphism between
(X,N_{A}^{c},A)
and
(X^{\prime},(N^{\prime})_{A}^{c},A^{\prime})
.
Thus, we obtain an isomorphism
between
\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}
and
\mathsf{DNF}^{\pi}
.

Write
\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{DNF}

such that the axioms in
\Ax
are satisfied when interpreting the
variables used in
\Ax
as clopens.
Write also
\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}:\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})

for the functor that sends a
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frame to its
underlying frame (viewed as a coalgebra).
If
\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}
is well defined, then it is automatically
a functor, because the additional (non-admissible) neighborhoods of

\sigma
-descriptive frames ensure that every morphism in
\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)

is in particular a neighborhood morphism between the underlying neighborhood frames.

While the introduction of
\sigma
-descriptive frames ensures that

\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}:\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)\to\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})

becomes a functor,
it only moves the problem of functoriality elsewhere.
Indeed, we are not guaranteed that the category
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})

is dual to
\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)
.

In Section
6.3
we will prove that whenever
\Ax
implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
,
then

(1)

\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)
is a category of coalgebras for an endofunctor

\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
on
\mathsf{Stone}
; and

(2)

\mathfun{N}_{\Ax}
is dual to
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
.

Combined, these give the dual equivalence

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax})\cong\mathsf{DNF}^{\sigma}(\Ax)
.

6.3.
When are
\Sigma
and
\Pi
functors?

In this section we give a sufficient condition for
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
to
be a functor.

{defi}

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Stone space with the underlying set
X
. We write

\mathfun{K}\mathbb{X}
and
\mathfun{O}\mathbb{X}
for the closed and open sets
of
\mathbb{X}
and define
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
to be the space consisting of

W\subseteq\mathfun{P}X
that satisfy

e\in W\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists c\in\mathfun{K}\mathbb{X},d\in\mathfun{O}\mathbb{X}\text{ with }c\subseteq e\subseteq d\text{ and }[c,d]\cap\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\subseteq W.

The topology on
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
is generated by the clopen subbase

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 0 L 3.57 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta=\{W\mid a\in W\},\qquad\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.52pt{\vbox to6.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.25974pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.21 0 L 4.21 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta=\{W\mid X\setminus a\notin W\},

where
a
ranges over
\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
.

Using the fact that elements of
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
are determined
uniquely by the clopens of
\mathbb{X}
they contain, combined with assignments
similar to the ones in the paragraph following Definition
6.2
,
it is easy to see that

\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
is homeomorphic to
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
.
Therefore,
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
is a Stone space.
Moreover,
W\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
is an
\Ax
-subset
(see Definition
5.2
) iff
W\cap\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\in\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
is an
\Ax
-subset.
Thus, we define:

{defi}

For a Stone space
\mathbb{X}
, let
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
be the
subspace of
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}\mathbb{X}
whose elements are
\Ax
-subsets.
For a continuous function
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
,
we define

\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f:\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}:W\mapsto\{w^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\mid f^{-1}(w^{\prime})\in W\}.

Since
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
is homeomorphic to

\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
, we have that
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}

sends a Stone space to a Stone space.
Furthermore, this implies that
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
is
naturally isomorphic to
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}

whenever the former is well defined
.
We prove that it is well defined when
\Ax
implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
.

Theorem 35
.

Let
\Ax
be a set of finitary one-step axioms such that

\Ax
implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
.
Then
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
defines an endofunctor on
\mathsf{Stone}
.

Proof 6.3
.

We have already seen that
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}
is well defined on
objects, and functoriality is straightforward because it is a subfunctor
of
\mathfun{B}
.
Therefore, we only have to prove that for every continuous function

f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, the assignment

\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f:\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}

is a well-defined continuous function.

Continuity follows from the fact that

(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f)^{-1}(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 0 L 3.57 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pta^{\prime})=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.6pt{\vbox to5.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.7986pt\lower-2.7986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 0 L 3.57 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptf^{-1}(a^{\prime})
for all

a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
.
So all that is left is to prove that

\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
for all

W\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}\mathbb{X}
.
That is, we need to show that for all
e^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
,

\begin{split}e^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)\quad\text{iff}\quad&\exists c^{\prime}\in\mathfun{K}\mathbb{X}^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in\mathfun{O}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\\
&\text{with }c^{\prime}\subseteq e^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}\text{ and }[c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]\cap\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W).\end{split}

(13)

First assume
e^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)
.
Then
f^{-1}(e^{\prime})\in W
and so there exist
c\in\mathfun{K}\mathbb{X}

and
d\in\mathfun{O}\mathbb{X}
such that
c\subseteq f^{-1}(e)\subseteq d

and
[c,d]\cap\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\subseteq W
.
Define
c^{\prime}=f[c]
and
d^{\prime}=\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\setminus f[\mathbb{X}\setminus d]
.
Since
f
is a continuous function between Stone spaces, it sends closed sets
to closed sets, so
c^{\prime}\in\mathfun{K}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}\in\mathfun{O}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, we claim that
c^{\prime}\subseteq e^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}
.
The first inclusion is obvious. For the second, if

x^{\prime}\notin d^{\prime}
, then there is
x\in\mathbb{X}\setminus d
such that

f(x)=x^{\prime}
. But then
x\notin d
, so
x\notin f^{-1}(e^{\prime})
, and hence
x^{\prime}=f(x)\notin e^{\prime}
.

We claim that
c^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}
witness that the right-hand side of
(
13
) holds.
Let
a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that
c^{\prime}\subseteq a^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}
.
Then
c\subseteq f^{-1}(a^{\prime})
by definition of
c^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
f^{-1}(d^{\prime})\subseteq d
.
To see this,
x\notin d
implies
x\in\mathbb{X}\setminus d
,
so
f(x)\in f[\mathbb{X}\setminus d]
. Therefore,

f(x)\notin\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\setminus f[\mathbb{X}\setminus d]=d^{\prime}
,
and hence
x\notin f^{-1}(d^{\prime})
.
Thus,
f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\subseteq d
.
By assumption this implies that
f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\in W
. Consequently,

a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)
.

For the converse, suppose
e^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
is such that the right-hand side holds.
Denote the relevant closed and open subsets of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
witnessing this
by
c^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}
.
We aim to show that
f^{-1}(e^{\prime})\in W
.
To prove this, it suffices to show that there exist a closed and open subsets

c
and
d
of
\mathbb{X}
such that
c\subseteq f^{-1}(e^{\prime})\subseteq d

and
[c,d]\cap\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}\subseteq W
.

Take
c=f^{-1}(c^{\prime})
and
d=f^{-1}(d^{\prime})
. By continuity of
f
these are
closed and open, respectively. Now let
a\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
be such that

c\subseteq a\subseteq d
.
To prove that
a\in W
, we construct
b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}

such that
b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)
and

f^{-1}(b_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq a\subseteq f^{-1}(b_{2}^{\prime})
.
Convexity of
W
then implies that
a\in W
.

To construct
b_{1}^{\prime}
, since
c^{\prime}
is closed, we have that

c^{\prime}=\bigcap\{k^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\mid c^{\prime}\subseteq k^{\prime}\}
.
Therefore,

c=f^{-1}(c^{\prime})=\bigcap\big\{f^{-1}(k^{\prime})\mid k^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\text{ and }c^{\prime}\subseteq k^{\prime}\big\}\subseteq a.

Since
a
is clopen and the intersection is directed, we can find

k_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that
c\subseteq f^{-1}(k_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq a
.
Similarly, since
c^{\prime}=\bigcap\{k^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\mid c^{\prime}\subseteq k^{\prime}\}\subseteq d^{\prime}
we can find

k_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that

c^{\prime}\subseteq k_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}
.
Setting
b_{1}^{\prime}=k_{1}^{\prime}\cap k_{2}^{\prime}
gives an element in

\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
such that
f^{-1}(b_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq a
.
Moreover, by construction
c^{\prime}\subseteq b_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}
,
and hence
b_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}f(W)
.

Finally, to construct
b_{2}^{\prime}
, since
d^{\prime}
is open, we have

d^{\prime}=\bigcup\{k^{\prime}\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\mid k^{\prime}\subseteq d^{\prime}\}
.
A similar argument to the above yields
b_{2}^{\prime}
satisfying all the required properties.
This proves that
a\in W
, hence
f^{-1}(e^{\prime})\in W
, completing the proof.

The definition of descriptive monotone frames from
[
HK04
]

coincides with the definition of
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frames
satisfying monotonicity. Let us write
\mathsf{HDMF}
for the category
of such descriptive monotone frames, as defined in Section 2.4.2 of
op. ​cit
.
The algebraic semantics of monotone modal logic is given by

monotone Boolean algebra expansions
(BAMs),
and we write
\mathsf{BAM}
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{NA}
whose objects
are BAMs
[
HK04
, Section 2.4.1]
.
As a consequence of Theorem
35
we
now obtain the duality from
[
HK04
, Theorem 2.11]

as an algebra/coalgebra duality.

Corollary 36

(Hansen-Kupke)
.

\mathsf{BAM}\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{HDMF}
.

Proof 6.4
.

One can prove that
\mathsf{BAM}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})
in the same way
as in Proposition
4
(
1
).
Moreover,
\mathsf{HDMF}\cong\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{UpV})

[
HK04
, Theorem 3.12]
, where

\mathfun{UpV}
is the endofunctor on
\mathsf{Stone}
defined in
Definition 3.9 of op. ​cit.
There is a natural isomorphism
\nu:\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}\to\mathfun{UpV}

given on components by

\nu_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{UpV}\mathbb{X}:W\mapsto\{c\in W\mid c\text{ is closed in }\mathbb{X}\}.

This is injective because if
W,V\in\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
are distinct,
then there exists
a\in\mathfun{clp}\mathbb{X}
such that
a\in W
and
a\notin V
,
or
a\notin W
and
a\in V
. Since clopen sets are in particular closed,
this implies that
\nu_{\mathbb{X}}(W)\neq\nu_{\mathbb{X}}(V)
, so
\nu_{\mathbb{X}}
is injective.
Moreover, it is surjective. To see this, observe that for all
W\in\mathfun{UpV}\mathbb{X}

the set
W^{\uparrow}=\{e\subseteq\mathbb{X}\mid\exists c\in W\text{ such that }c\subseteq e\}

is in
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}\mathbb{X}
and satisfies
\nu_{\mathbb{X}}(W^{\uparrow})=W
.
So
\nu
is a bijective continuous function, hence a homeomorphism.

Combining Theorems
29

and
35
yields that
\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}}
is dual
to
\mathfun{UpV}
. Together with known isomorphisms this yields

\mathsf{BAM}\cong\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{UpV})\cong\mathsf{HDMF},

which proves the desired duality.

Corollary 37
.

Let
\Ax
be a collection of finitary one-step axioms that implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
.
Then

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}).

Proof 6.5
.

Combine Corollary
30
, Theorem
35
,
and the fact that
\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}

to obtain

\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax})\cong\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax}).

Corollary 38
.

Let
\Ax
be a collection of finitary one-step axioms that implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
.
If the restriction
\Sigma_{\Ax}
of
\Sigma
to
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax})

lands in
\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})
,
then
\Sigma_{\Ax}
is a functor.

Proof 6.6
.

If
\Sigma_{\Ax}
is well defined, then so is
\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}
, and
hence
\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}
is a functor (as we pointed out after Remark
34
).
Since
\Sigma_{\Ax}
can be obtained as the composition of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax}^{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\Ax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}}

it is a functor as well.

We can now obtain relatively easily the commuting diagrams relating
the Jónsson-Tarski type and Thomason type dualities for
a large class of modal logics.
These are analogues of the diagrams for basic normal modal logic depicted
in (
1
).
We formulate this as a general statement, and then instantiate it to
basic monotone modal logic.
Observe that this still requires a preservation result reminiscent
of a Sahlqvist theorem, proving that validity of axioms on a
\sigma
-descriptive neighborhood frame implies validity of the axioms on the underlying neighborhood frame (see, e.g.,
[
SV89
]
or
[
BRV01
, Section 5.6]
). We leave the search for such theorems
to future research.

Theorem 39
.

Let
\Ax
be a collection of finitary one-step axioms that implies
\mathsf{(Conv)}
.
Suppose that for every Stone space
\mathbb{X}
,
every
\Ax
-neighborhood
W
of
\mathfun{D}\mathbb{X}
is also an

\Ax
-neighborhood of
\mathfun{B}X
, where
X
is the set underlying
\mathbb{X}
.
Then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\Ax})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\Ax})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\Ax})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}_{\Ax})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\Ax})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\Ax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}_{\Ax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}

{exa}

As we have seen, examples of such
\Ax
are
\Ax=\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:conv}{$\mathsf{(Conv)}$}}\}

and
\Ax=\{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}\}
.
The latter yields the following commuting diagrams, where all
edges are functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{N}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}^{\sigma}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{BAM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{D}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HDMF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Alg}(\mathfun{L}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coalg}(\mathfun{B}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\mathsf{(M)}$}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CABAM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MF}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{(M)}$}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{(M)}$}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\text{\hyperlink{ax:M}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{(M)}$}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfun{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv^{\op}}

7.
Conclusions

We have given a general coalgebraic approach to Thomason type dualities for
neighborhood frames and Jónsson-Tarski type dualities for neighborhood
algebras. Furthermore, we have investigated the relationship between the two
types of dualities via the theory of canonical extensions.
We list several potential avenues for future research.

Infinitary modal logic:

The Thomason type dualities from Sections
3

and
4
provide dualities for algebraic and geometric
semantics for
infinitary
modal logic. While some interesting
investigations have been conducted by Baltag
[
Bal98
,
Bal00
]
,
obtaining a more general coalgebraic approach towards infinitary modal
logic (also using the results of this paper) remains open.

Endofunctors as left adjoints:

In
[
BCM22
]
the functor
\mathfun{H}
is obtained as the
left adjoint of the forgetful functor from
\mathsf{CABA}
to

\mathsf{CSL}
—the category of complete meet-semilattices.
In a similar way we can obtain the functor whose coalgebras are
monotone neighborhood frames as the left adjoint of the forgetful
functor
\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{Pos}
,
and the functor whose coalgebras are filter frames arises as the
left adjoint of
\mathsf{CABA}\to\mathsf{SL}
, where
\mathsf{SL}
is
the category of (not necessarily complete) meet-semilattices.
An interesting direction for future work is to investigate the
connection between presentations of classes of algebras via adjoints
of forgetful functors and via one-step axioms of infinitary logic.

When are
\Sigma
and
\Pi
functors? A different approach:

In Section
6.3
we proved that in presence of
the convexity axiom,
\Sigma
defines a functor.
Another approach towards the same goal is by modifying the morphisms
between (descriptive) neighborhood frames.
If we define a
\sigma
-morphism to be a function
between neighborhood frames that satisfies only the left-to-right
implication in (
3
),
then replacing neighborhood morphisms with
\sigma
-morphisms ensures
that
\Sigma
is a functor without adding any additional axioms.
Considering such morphisms is natural for a number of reasons.
For example, in the special case of topological spaces they simply
correspond to continuous maps. They also generalize the stable
morphisms studied in
[
BBI16
,
BBI18
]
to the setting of (descriptive)
neighborhood frames. We plan to investigate this topic further in a
sequel to this paper.
